Fuel shortages caused long lines of cars at petrol stations. Inflation had driven the price of pasta (ordinary dry Italian noodles, a staple at that time) to five U.S. dollars per kilogram. The price of <a href="/wiki/Khat" title="Khat">khat</a>, imported daily from Kenya, was also five U.S. dollars per standard bunch. Paper currency notes were of such low value that several bundles were needed to pay for simple restaurant meals.
</p><p>A thriving black market existed in the centre of the city as banks experienced shortages of local currency for exchange. At night, the city of Mogadishu lay in darkness. Close monitoring of all visiting foreigners was in effect. Harsh <a href="/wiki/Exchange_control" class="mw-redirect" title="Exchange control">exchange control</a> regulations were introduced to prevent export of foreign currency. Although no travel restrictions were placed on foreigners, photographing many locations was banned. During daytime in Mogadishu, the appearance of any government military force was extremely rare. Alleged late-night operations by government authorities, however, included "disappearances" of individuals from their homes.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1991, the Barre administration was ousted by a coalition of clan-based opposition groups, backed by Ethiopia's then-ruling <a href="/wiki/Derg" title="Derg">Derg</a> regime and <a href="/wiki/Great_Socialist_People%27s_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Socialist People&#39;s Libyan Arab Jamahiriya">Libya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Following a meeting of the <a href="/wiki/Somali_National_Movement" title="Somali National Movement">Somali National Movement</a> and northern clans' elders, the northern former British portion of the country <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_Declaration_of_Independence" title="Somaliland Declaration of Independence">declared its independence</a> as the <a href="/wiki/Somaliland" title="Somaliland">Republic of Somaliland</a> in May 1991. Although <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">de facto</i></span> independent and relatively stable compared to the tumultuous south, it has not been recognized by any foreign government.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mogadishu.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Mogadishu.jpg/220px-Mogadishu.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Mogadishu.jpg/330px-Mogadishu.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Mogadishu.jpg/440px-Mogadishu.jpg 2x" data-file-width="460" data-file-height="268" /></a><figcaption>Prior to the civil war, Mogadishu was known as the "White pearl of the Indian Ocean".<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Many of the opposition groups subsequently began competing for influence in the power vacuum that followed the ouster of Barre's regime. In the south, armed factions led by USC commanders General <a href="/wiki/Mohamed_Farah_Aidid" class="mw-redirect" title="Mohamed Farah Aidid">Mohamed Farah Aidid</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ali_Mahdi_Mohamed" class="mw-redirect" title="Ali Mahdi Mohamed">Ali Mahdi Mohamed</a>, in particular, clashed as each sought to exert authority over the capital.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> In 1991, a multi-phased international conference on Somalia was held in neighbouring Djibouti. Aidid boycotted the first meeting in protest.<sup id="cite_ref-Paul_Fricska_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paul_Fricska-156">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Owing to the legitimacy bestowed on Muhammad by the Djibouti conference, he was subsequently recognized by the international community as the new President of Somalia. Djibouti, <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saudi_Arabia" title="Saudi Arabia">Saudi Arabia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> were among the countries that officially extended recognition to Muhammad's administration.<sup id="cite_ref-Paul_Fricska_156-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Paul_Fricska-156">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> He was not able to exert his authority beyond parts of the capital. Power was instead vied with other faction leaders in the southern half of Somalia and with autonomous sub-national entities in the north.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> The Djibouti conference was followed by two abortive agreements for national reconciliation and disarmament, which were signed by 15 political stakeholders: an agreement to hold an Informal Preparatory Meeting on National Reconciliation, and the 1993 Addis Ababa Agreement made at the Conference on National Reconciliation.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In the early 1990s, due to the protracted lack of a permanent central authority, Somalia began to be characterized as a "<a href="/wiki/Failed_state" title="Failed state">failed state</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Political scientist <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_Menkhaus" title="Kenneth Menkhaus">Ken Menkhaus</a> argues that evidence suggested that the nation had already attained failed state status by the mid-1980s,<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/Robert_I._Rotberg" title="Robert I. Rotberg">Robert I. Rotberg</a> similarly posits that the state failure had preceded the ouster of the Barre administration.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Hoehne (2009), Branwen (2009) and Verhoeven (2009) also used Somalia during this period as a case study to critique various aspects of the "state failure" discourse.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transitional_institutions">Transitional institutions</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Transitional_National_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional National Government">Transitional National Government</a>, <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Institutions" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Institutions">Transitional Federal Institutions</a>, <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Government">Transitional Federal Government</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Parliament">Transitional Federal Parliament</a></div>
<p>The Transitional National Government (TNG) was established in April–May 2000 at the Somalia National Peace Conference (SNPC) held in Arta, Djibouti. <a href="/wiki/Abdiqasim_Salad_Hassan" title="Abdiqasim Salad Hassan">Abdiqasim Salad Hassan</a> was selected as the President of the nation's new Transitional National Government (TNG), an interim administration formed to guide Somalia to its third permanent republican government.<sup id="cite_ref-Cfbsom_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cfbsom-164">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
The TNG's internal problems led to the replacement of the Prime Minister four times in three years, and the administrative body's reported bankruptcy in December 2003. Its mandate ended at the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-TNG-PRIME-MINISTER-CONCLUDES-FORMATION-OF-CABINET_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TNG-PRIME-MINISTER-CONCLUDES-FORMATION-OF-CABINET-165">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 10 October 2004, legislators elected <a href="/wiki/Abdullahi_Yusuf_Ahmed" title="Abdullahi Yusuf Ahmed">Abdullahi Yusuf Ahmed</a> as the first President of the <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Government">Transitional Federal Government</a> (TFG), the Transitional National Government's successor.<sup id="cite_ref-Spmoarc_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spmoarc-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> the TFG was the second interim administration aiming to restore national institutions to Somalia after the 1991 collapse of the Siad Barre regime and the ensuing civil war.<sup id="cite_ref-fctbk_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fctbk-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Transitional Federal Government (TFG) was the internationally recognised government of Somalia until 20 August 2012, when its tenure officially ended.<sup id="cite_ref-Fcacsunesinpshm2_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fcacsunesinpshm2-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> It was established as one of the <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Institutions" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Institutions">Transitional Federal Institutions</a> (TFIs) of government as defined in the <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Charter" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Charter">Transitional Federal Charter</a> (TFC) adopted in November 2004 by the <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional Federal Parliament">Transitional Federal Parliament</a> (TFP). The Transitional Federal Government officially comprised the <a href="/wiki/Executive_(government)" title="Executive (government)">executive</a> branch of government, with the TFP serving as the <a href="/wiki/Legislature" title="Legislature">legislative</a> branch. The government was headed by the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Somalia" title="President of Somalia">President of Somalia</a>, to whom the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_(government)" title="Cabinet (government)">cabinet</a> reported through the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Somalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Somalia">Prime Minister</a>. However, it was also used as a general term to refer to all three branches collectively.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Islamic_Courts_Union">Islamic Courts Union</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Islamic_Courts_Union" title="Islamic Courts Union">Islamic Courts Union</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Icu_somalia_map.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Icu_somalia_map.png/300px-Icu_somalia_map.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="389" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Icu_somalia_map.png/450px-Icu_somalia_map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Icu_somalia_map.png/600px-Icu_somalia_map.png 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3112" /></a><figcaption>Map showing the ICU at the peak of its influence.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2006, the <a href="/wiki/Islamic_Courts_Union" title="Islamic Courts Union">Islamic Courts Union</a> (ICU), assumed control of much of the southern part of the country for 6 months and imposed <a href="/wiki/Shari%27a" class="mw-redirect" title="Shari&#39;a">Shari'a</a> law. Top UN officials have referred to this brief period as a 'Golden era' in the history of Somali politics.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transitional_Federal_Government">Transitional Federal Government</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Somalia_War_(2006%E2%80%932009)" class="mw-redirect" title="Somalia War (2006–2009)">Somalia War (2006–2009)</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Mogadishu_(2006)" title="Battle of Mogadishu (2006)">Battle of Mogadishu (2006)</a></div>
<p>The Transitional Federal Government sought to re-establish its authority, and, with the assistance of <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_National_Defense_Force" title="Ethiopian National Defense Force">Ethiopian troops</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> peacekeepers and air support by the United States, drove out the ICU and solidified its rule.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> On 8 January 2007, TFG President <a href="/wiki/Abdullahi_Yusuf_Ahmed" title="Abdullahi Yusuf Ahmed">Abdullahi Yusuf Ahmed</a>, entered Mogadishu with the Ethiopian military support for the first time since being elected to office. The government then relocated to <a href="/wiki/Villa_Somalia" title="Villa Somalia">Villa Somalia</a> in the capital from its interim location in <a href="/wiki/Baidoa" title="Baidoa">Baidoa</a>. This marked the first time since the fall of the Siad Barre regime in 1991 that the federal government controlled most of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-Gosppsdott_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gosppsdott-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Al_Shabaab_insurgency">Al Shabaab insurgency</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Al-Shabaab_(militant_group)" title="Al-Shabaab (militant group)">Al-Shabaab (militant group)</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Al-Shabaab_(Somalia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Shabaab (Somalia)">Al-Shabaab</a> opposed the Ethiopian military's presence in Somalia and continued an insurgency against the TFG. Throughout 2007 and 2008, Al-Shabaab scored military victories, seizing control of key towns and ports in both central and southern Somalia. By January 2009, Al-Shabaab and other militias had forced the Ethiopian troops to retreat, leaving behind an under-equipped African Union peacekeeping force to assist the Transitional Federal Government's troops.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Owing to a lack of funding and human resources, an <a href="/wiki/Arms_embargo" title="Arms embargo">arms embargo</a> that made it difficult to re-establish a national security force, and general indifference on the part of the international community, Yusuf found himself obliged to deploy thousands of troops from Puntland to Mogadishu to sustain the battle against insurgent elements in the southern part of the country. Financial support for this effort was provided by the autonomous region's government. This left little revenue for Puntland's own security forces and civil service employees, leaving the territory vulnerable to piracy and terrorist attacks.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 29 December 2008, Yusuf announced before a united parliament in Baidoa his resignation as President of Somalia. In his speech, which was broadcast on national radio, Yusuf expressed regret at failing to end the country's seventeen-year conflict as his government had been mandated to do.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> He also blamed the international community for their failure to support the government, and said that the speaker of parliament would succeed him in office per the <a href="/wiki/Transitional_Federal_Charter_of_the_Somali_Republic" title="Transitional Federal Charter of the Somali Republic">Charter of the Transitional Federal Government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="End_of_transitional_period">End of transitional period</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Hizbul_Islam" title="Hizbul Islam">Hizbul Islam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ahlu_Sunna_Waljama%27a" title="Ahlu Sunna Waljama&#39;a">Ahlu Sunna Waljama'a</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alliance_for_the_Re-liberation_of_Somalia" title="Alliance for the Re-liberation of Somalia">Alliance for the Re-liberation of Somalia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War_(2009%E2%80%93present)" title="Somali Civil War (2009–present)">Somali Civil War (2009–present)</a>, and <a href="/wiki/2009_timeline_of_the_War_in_Somalia" class="mw-redirect" title="2009 timeline of the War in Somalia">2009 timeline of the War in Somalia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg/220px-Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg/330px-Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg/440px-Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed.jpg 2x" data-file-width="643" data-file-height="509" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed" title="Sharif Sheikh Ahmed">Sharif Sheikh Ahmed</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Between 31 May and 9 June 2008, representatives of Somalia's federal government and the Alliance for the Re-liberation of Somalia (ARS) participated in peace talks in Djibouti brokered by the former United Nations Special Envoy to Somalia, <a href="/wiki/Ahmedou_Ould-Abdallah" title="Ahmedou Ould-Abdallah">Ahmedou Ould-Abdallah</a>. The conference ended with a signed agreement calling for the withdrawal of Ethiopian troops in exchange for the cessation of armed confrontation. Parliament was subsequently expanded to 550 seats to accommodate ARS members, which then elected <a href="/wiki/Sheikh_Sharif_Sheikh_Ahmed" class="mw-redirect" title="Sheikh Sharif Sheikh Ahmed">Sheikh Sharif Sheikh Ahmed</a>, as president.<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Somali_Civil_War_(2009-present).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="SVG map showing relative control of the central government, Somaliland, and other actors" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg/220px-Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="279" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg/330px-Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg/440px-Somali_Civil_War_%282009-present%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="650" /></a><figcaption>Political and military situation in Somalia as of March 2023</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the help of a small team of African Union troops, the TFG began a <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War_(2009%E2%80%93present)" title="Somali Civil War (2009–present)">counteroffensive</a> in February 2009 to assume full control of the southern half of the country. To solidify its rule, the TFG formed an alliance with the Islamic Courts Union, other members of the Alliance for the Re-liberation of Somalia, and <a href="/wiki/Ahlu_Sunna_Waljama%27a" title="Ahlu Sunna Waljama&#39;a">Ahlu Sunna Waljama'a</a>, a moderate <a href="/wiki/Sufism" title="Sufism">Sufi</a> militia.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, Al-Shabaab and Hizbul Islam, the two main Islamist groups in opposition, began to fight amongst themselves in mid-2009.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> As a truce, in March 2009, the TFG announced that it would re-implement Shari'a as the nation's official judicial system.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> However, conflict continued in the southern and central parts of the country. Within months, the TFG had gone from holding about 70% of south-central Somalia's conflict zones, to losing control of over 80% of the disputed territory to the Islamist insurgents.<sup id="cite_ref-Gosppsdott_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gosppsdott-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2011, a coordinated operation, <a href="/wiki/Operation_Linda_Nchi" title="Operation Linda Nchi">Operation Linda Nchi</a> between the Somali and Kenyan militaries and multinational forces began against the Al-Shabaab in southern Somalia.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> By September 2012, Somali, Kenyan, and <a href="/wiki/Raskamboni_movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Raskamboni movement">Raskamboni</a> forces had managed to capture Al-Shabaab's last major stronghold, the southern port of Kismayo.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> In July 2012, three European Union operations were launched to engage with Somalia: <a href="/wiki/European_Union_Training_Mission_Somalia" class="mw-redirect" title="European Union Training Mission Somalia">EUTM Somalia</a>, EU Naval Force Somalia <a href="/wiki/Operation_Atalanta" title="Operation Atalanta">Operation Atalanta</a> off the Horn of Africa, and EUCAP Nestor.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg/220px-Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="113" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg/330px-Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg/440px-Parlament_of_Somalia_2012-2016.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="360" data-file-height="185" /></a><figcaption>Structure of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Parliament_of_Somalia" title="Federal Parliament of Somalia">Federal Parliament of Somalia</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As part of the official "Roadmap for the End of Transition", a political process that provided clear benchmarks leading toward the formation of permanent democratic institutions in Somalia, the Transitional Federal Government's interim mandate ended on 20 August 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-Aasratdocwau2_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aasratdocwau2-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Federal_Parliament_of_Somalia" title="Federal Parliament of Somalia">Federal Parliament of Somalia</a> was concurrently inaugurated.<sup id="cite_ref-Fcacsunesinpshm2_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fcacsunesinpshm2-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Federal_government">Federal government</span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Federal_Government_of_Somalia" title="Federal Government of Somalia">Federal Government of Somalia</a>, the first permanent central government in the country since the start of the civil war, was established in August 2012. In August 2014, the Somali government-led <a href="/wiki/Operation_Indian_Ocean" title="Operation Indian Ocean">Operation Indian Ocean</a> was launched against insurgent-held pockets in the countryside.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Somalia" title="Geography of Somalia">Geography of Somalia</a></div>
<p>Somalia is bordered by <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a> to the west, the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Aden" title="Gulf of Aden">Gulf of Aden</a> to the north, the Somali Sea and <a href="/wiki/Guardafui_Channel" title="Guardafui Channel">Guardafui Channel</a> to the east, and <a href="/wiki/Kenya" title="Kenya">Kenya</a> to the southwest. With a land area of 637,657 square kilometers, Somalia's terrain consists mainly of <a href="/wiki/Plateau" title="Plateau">plateaus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plain" title="Plain">plains</a> and <a href="/wiki/Highland" title="Highland">highlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-185">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Coastline_of_Somalia" title="Coastline of Somalia">Its coastline</a> is more than 3,333 kilometers in length, the longest of mainland Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-FactbookCoastline_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FactbookCoastline-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> It has been described as being roughly shaped "like a tilted number seven".<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the far north, the rugged east–west ranges of the <a href="/wiki/Ogo_Mountains" title="Ogo Mountains">Ogo Mountains</a> lie at varying distances from the Gulf of Aden coast. Hot conditions prevail year-round, along with periodic <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoon</a> winds and irregular rainfall.<sup id="cite_ref-ClimateSom_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ClimateSom-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Geology suggests the presence of valuable mineral deposits. Somalia is separated from <a href="/wiki/Seychelles" title="Seychelles">Seychelles</a> by the Somali Sea and is separated from <a href="/wiki/Socotra" title="Socotra">Socotra</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Guardafui_Channel" title="Guardafui Channel">Guardafui Channel</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Somalia" title="Administrative divisions of Somalia">Administrative divisions of Somalia</a> and <a href="/wiki/States_and_regions_of_Somalia" title="States and regions of Somalia">States and regions of Somalia</a></div>
<p>Somalia is officially divided into thirteen <a href="/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Somalia" title="Administrative divisions of Somalia">regions</a> and five claimed regions (<i>gobollada</i>, singular <i>gobol</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> which in turn are subdivided into districts. The regions are:
</p>

<p>Northern Somalia is now <i>de facto</i> divided up among the <a href="/wiki/States_and_regions_of_Somalia" title="States and regions of Somalia">autonomous regions</a> of <a href="/wiki/Puntland" title="Puntland">Puntland</a> (which considers itself an <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_state" class="mw-redirect" title="Autonomous state">autonomous state</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Somaliland" title="Somaliland">Somaliland</a> (a self-declared but <a href="/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition" title="List of states with limited recognition">unrecognized sovereign state</a>). In central Somalia, <a href="/wiki/Galmudug" title="Galmudug">Galmudug</a> is another regional entity that emerged just south of Puntland. <a href="/wiki/Jubaland" title="Jubaland">Jubaland</a> in the far south is a fourth autonomous region within the federation.<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In 2014, a new <a href="/wiki/South_West_State_of_Somalia" title="South West State of Somalia">Southwestern Somalia</a> was likewise established.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> In April 2015, a formation conference was also launched for a new <a href="/wiki/Central_Regions_State" title="Central Regions State">Central Regions State</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Federal Parliament is tasked with selecting the ultimate number and boundaries of the autonomous regional states (officially <i>Federal Member States</i>) within the Federal Republic of Somalia.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Location">Location</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Almadow_Overview.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Almadow_Overview.JPG/220px-Almadow_Overview.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Almadow_Overview.JPG/330px-Almadow_Overview.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Almadow_Overview.JPG/440px-Almadow_Overview.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Cal_Madow" title="Cal Madow">Cal Madow</a> mountain range in Somaliland features the nation's highest peak, <a href="/wiki/Shimbiris" class="mw-redirect" title="Shimbiris">Shimbiris</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<div class="thumb tnone" style="margin-left:auto;margin-right:auto;overflow:hidden;width:auto;max-width:308px"><div class="thumbinner"><div class="noresize" style="overflow:auto"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Baalade Valley near Bendergazim photographed by French explorer &amp; diplomat Georges Revoil in 1881"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg/300px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="117" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg/450px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg/600px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="273" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bedding_van_de_rivier_Balada_in_de_omgeving_van_Bender_Gasem_TMnr_60027146.jpg" title="File:COLLECTIE TROPENMUSEUM Bedding van de rivier Balada in de omgeving van Bender Gasem TMnr 60027146.jpg"> </a></div><a href="/wiki/Baalade" title="Baalade">Baalade</a> Valley near Bendergazim photographed by French explorer &amp; diplomat Georges Revoil in 1881</div></div></div>
<p>Somalia is bordered by Kenya to the southwest, the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Aden" title="Gulf of Aden">Gulf of Aden</a> to the north, the <a href="/wiki/Guardafui_Channel" title="Guardafui Channel">Guardafui Channel</a> and Indian Ocean to the east, and Ethiopia to the west. The country claims a border with <a href="/wiki/Djibouti" title="Djibouti">Djibouti</a> through the disputed territory of <a href="/wiki/Somaliland" title="Somaliland">Somaliland</a> to the northwest. It lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/2nd_parallel_south" title="2nd parallel south">2°S</a> and <a href="/wiki/12th_parallel_north" title="12th parallel north">12°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/41st_meridian_east" title="41st meridian east">41°</a> and <a href="/wiki/52nd_meridian_east" title="52nd meridian east">52°E</a>. Strategically located at the mouth of the <a href="/wiki/Bab_el_Mandeb" class="mw-redirect" title="Bab el Mandeb">Bab el Mandeb</a> gateway to the <a href="/wiki/Red_Sea" title="Red Sea">Red Sea</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, the country occupies the tip of a region that, due to its resemblance on the map to a <a href="/wiki/Rhinoceros" title="Rhinoceros">rhinoceros</a>' horn, is commonly referred to as the Horn of Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Waters">Waters</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Islands_of_Somalia" title="Islands of Somalia">Islands of Somalia</a></div>
<p>Somalia has the longest coastline on the mainland of Africa,<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> with a seaboard that stretches 3,333 kilometres (2,071&#160;mi). Its terrain consists mainly of <a href="/wiki/Plateau" title="Plateau">plateaus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plain" title="Plain">plains</a> and <a href="/wiki/Highland" title="Highland">highlands</a>. The nation has a total area of 637,657 square kilometres (246,201&#160;sq&#160;mi) of which constitutes land, with 10,320 square kilometres (3,980&#160;sq&#160;mi) of water. Somalia's land boundaries extend to about 2,340 kilometres (1,450&#160;mi); 58 kilometres (36&#160;mi) of that is shared with Djibouti, 682 kilometres (424&#160;mi) with Kenya, and 1,626 kilometres (1,010&#160;mi) with Ethiopia. Its maritime claims include <a href="/wiki/Territorial_waters" title="Territorial waters">territorial waters</a> of 200 nautical miles (370&#160;km; 230&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somalia has several islands and archipelagos on its coast, including the <a href="/wiki/Bajuni_Islands" title="Bajuni Islands">Bajuni Islands</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Saad_ad-Din_Islands" class="mw-redirect" title="Saad ad-Din Islands">Saad ad-Din Archipelago</a>: see <a href="/wiki/Islands_of_Somalia" title="Islands of Somalia">islands of Somalia</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg/220px-Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg/330px-Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg/440px-Juba_river_downstream_Jamaame.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Jubba_River" title="Jubba River">Jubba River</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Habitat">Habitat</span></h3>
<p>Somalia contains seven terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_montane_forests" title="Ethiopian montane forests">Ethiopian montane forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northern_Zanzibar%E2%80%93Inhambane_coastal_forest_mosaic" title="Northern Zanzibar–Inhambane coastal forest mosaic">Northern Zanzibar–Inhambane coastal forest mosaic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_Acacia%E2%80%93Commiphora_bushlands_and_thickets" title="Somali Acacia–Commiphora bushlands and thickets">Somali Acacia–Commiphora bushlands and thickets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_xeric_grasslands_and_shrublands" title="Ethiopian xeric grasslands and shrublands">Ethiopian xeric grasslands and shrublands</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hobyo_grasslands_and_shrublands" title="Hobyo grasslands and shrublands">Hobyo grasslands and shrublands</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_montane_xeric_woodlands" title="Somali montane xeric woodlands">Somali montane xeric woodlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/East_African_mangroves" title="East African mangroves">East African mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the north, a scrub-covered, semi-desert plain referred as the <i><a href="/wiki/Guban" title="Guban">Guban</a></i> lies parallel to the Gulf of Aden <a href="/wiki/Littoral_zone" title="Littoral zone">littoral</a>. With a width of twelve kilometres in the west to as little as two kilometres in the east, the plain is bisected by watercourses that are essentially beds of dry sand except during the rainy seasons. When the rains arrive, the Guban's low bushes and grass clumps transform into lush vegetation.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> This coastal strip is part of the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_xeric_grasslands_and_shrublands" title="Ethiopian xeric grasslands and shrublands">Ethiopian xeric grasslands and shrublands</a> <a href="/wiki/Ecoregion" title="Ecoregion">ecoregion</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Cal_Madow" title="Cal Madow">Cal Madow</a> is a <a href="/wiki/Mountain_range" title="Mountain range">mountain range</a> in the northeastern part of the country. Extending from several kilometres west of the city of <a href="/wiki/Bosaso" title="Bosaso">Bosaso</a> to the northwest of <a href="/wiki/Erigavo" title="Erigavo">Erigavo</a>, it features Somalia's highest <a href="/wiki/Summit_(topography)" class="mw-redirect" title="Summit (topography)">peak</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shimbiris" class="mw-redirect" title="Shimbiris">Shimbiris</a>, which sits at an elevation of about 2,416 metres (7,927&#160;ft).<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The rugged east–west ranges of the Karkaar Mountains also lie to the interior of the Gulf of Aden littoral.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In the central regions, the country's northern mountain ranges give way to shallow plateaus and typically dry watercourses that are referred to locally as the <i>Ogo</i>. The Ogo's western plateau, in turn, gradually merges into the <a href="/wiki/Haud" title="Haud">Haud</a>, an important grazing area for livestock.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somalia has only two permanent rivers, the <a href="/wiki/Jubba_River" title="Jubba River">Jubba</a> and <a href="/wiki/Shebelle_River" title="Shebelle River">Shabele</a>, both of which begin in the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Highlands" title="Ethiopian Highlands">Ethiopian Highlands</a>. These rivers mainly flow southwards, with the Jubba River entering the Indian Ocean at <a href="/wiki/Kismayo" title="Kismayo">Kismayo</a>. The Shabele River at one time apparently used to enter the sea near <a href="/wiki/Merca" title="Merca">Merca</a>, but now reaches a point just southwest of Mogadishu. After that, it consists of swamps and dry reaches before finally disappearing in the desert terrain east of <a href="/wiki/Jilib" title="Jilib">Jilib</a>, near the Jubba River.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environment">Environment</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Somcoralreef.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Somcoralreef.jpg/300px-Somcoralreef.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="443" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Somcoralreef.jpg/450px-Somcoralreef.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Somcoralreef.jpg/600px-Somcoralreef.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1320" data-file-height="1949" /></a><figcaption>Somalia's <a href="/wiki/Coral_reef" title="Coral reef">coral reefs</a>, ecological parks and protected areas</figcaption></figure>
<p>Somalia is a <a href="/wiki/Semi-arid_climate" title="Semi-arid climate">semi-arid</a> country with about 1.64% <a href="/wiki/Arable_land" title="Arable land">arable land</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_1-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The first local environmental organizations were Ecoterra Somalia and the Somali Ecological Society, both of which helped promote awareness about ecological concerns and mobilized environmental programs in all governmental sectors as well as in civil society. From 1971 onward, a massive tree-planting campaign on a nationwide scale was introduced by the Siad Barre government to halt the advance of thousands of acres of wind-driven <a href="/wiki/Sand_dune" class="mw-redirect" title="Sand dune">sand dunes</a> that threatened to engulf towns, roads and farm land.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> By 1988, 265 hectares of a projected 336 hectares had been treated, with 39 range reserve sites and 36 forestry plantation sites established.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In 1986, the Wildlife Rescue, Research and Monitoring Centre was established by Ecoterra International, with the goal of sensitizing the public to ecological issues. This educational effort led in 1989 to the so-called "Somalia proposal" and a decision by the Somali government to adhere to the <a href="/wiki/CITES" title="CITES">Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora</a> (CITES), which established for the first time a worldwide ban on the trade of elephant <a href="/wiki/Ivory" title="Ivory">ivory</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_(8071381265).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg/220px-Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="139" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg/330px-Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg/440px-Aerial_views_of_Kismayo_06_%288071381265%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3322" data-file-height="2099" /></a><figcaption>The coast south of Mogadishu</figcaption></figure>
<p>Later, <a href="/wiki/Fatima_Jibrell" title="Fatima Jibrell">Fatima Jibrell</a>, a prominent Somali environmental activist, mounted a successful campaign to conserve old-growth forests of <a href="/wiki/Acacia" title="Acacia">acacia</a> trees in the northeastern part of Somalia.<sup id="cite_ref-Gilbert_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gilbert-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> These trees, which can live for 500 years, were being cut down to make charcoal which was highly in demand in the Arabian Peninsula, where the region's <a href="/wiki/Bedouin" title="Bedouin">Bedouin</a> tribes believe the acacia to be sacred.<sup id="cite_ref-Gilbert_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gilbert-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-IWD_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IWD-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> However, while being a relatively inexpensive fuel that meets a user's needs, the production of charcoal often leads to <a href="/wiki/Deforestation" title="Deforestation">deforestation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Desertification" title="Desertification">desertification</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-IWD_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IWD-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> As a way of addressing this problem, Jibrell and the Horn of Africa Relief and Development Organization (Horn Relief; now <a href="/wiki/Adeso" title="Adeso">Adeso</a>), an organization of which she was the founder and executive director, trained a group of teens to educate the public on the permanent damage that producing charcoal can create. In 1999, Horn Relief coordinated a peace march in the northeastern Puntland region of Somalia to put an end to the so-called "charcoal wars". As a result of Jibrell's lobbying and education efforts, the Puntland government in 2000 prohibited the exportation of charcoal. The government has also since enforced the ban, which has reportedly led to an 80% drop in exports of the product.<sup id="cite_ref-Goldman_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goldman-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> Jibrell was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Goldman_Environmental_Prize" title="Goldman Environmental Prize">Goldman Environmental Prize</a> in 2002 for her efforts against environmental degradation and desertification.<sup id="cite_ref-Goldman_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goldman-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> In 2008, she also won the <a href="/wiki/National_Geographic_Society" title="National Geographic Society">National Geographic Society</a>/<a href="/wiki/Buffett_Foundation" title="Buffett Foundation">Buffett Foundation</a> Award for Leadership in Conservation.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the massive <a href="/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake" class="mw-redirect" title="2004 Indian Ocean earthquake">tsunami of December 2004</a>, there have also emerged allegations that after the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War" title="Somali Civil War">Somali Civil War</a> in the late 1980s, Somalia's long, remote shoreline was used as a dump site for the disposal of toxic waste. The huge waves that battered northern Somalia after the tsunami are believed to have stirred up tons of nuclear and toxic waste that might have been dumped illegally in the country by foreign firms.<sup id="cite_ref-Toxic_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Toxic-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/European_Green_Party" title="European Green Party">European Green Party</a> followed up these revelations by presenting before the press and the <a href="/wiki/European_Parliament" title="European Parliament">European Parliament</a> in <a href="/wiki/Strasbourg" title="Strasbourg">Strasbourg</a> copies of contracts signed by two European companies — the Italian Swiss firm, Achair Partners, and an Italian <a href="/wiki/Waste_broker" title="Waste broker">waste broker</a>, Progresso — and representatives of the then President of Somalia, the faction leader Ali Mahdi Mohamed, to accept 10 million tonnes of toxic waste in exchange for $80 million (then about £60 million).<sup id="cite_ref-Toxic_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Toxic-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to reports by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme" title="United Nations Environment Programme">United Nations Environment Programme</a> (UNEP), the waste has resulted in far higher than normal cases of respiratory infections, mouth ulcers and bleeding, abdominal haemorrhages and unusual skin infections among many inhabitants of the areas around the northeastern towns of <a href="/wiki/Hobyo" title="Hobyo">Hobyo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Benadir" class="mw-redirect" title="Benadir">Benadir</a> on the Indian Ocean coast — diseases consistent with radiation sickness. UNEP adds that the situation along the Somali coastline poses a very serious environmental hazard not only in Somalia, but also in the eastern Africa sub-region.<sup id="cite_ref-Toxic_200-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Toxic-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/300px-Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/450px-Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/600px-Somalia_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1900" data-file-height="1900" /></a><figcaption>Somalia map of Köppen climate classification.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Somalia" title="Climate change in Somalia">Climate change in Somalia</a></div>
<p>Owing to Somalia's proximity to the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">equator</a>, there is not much seasonal variation in its climate. Hot conditions prevail year-round along with periodic <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoon</a> winds and irregular rainfall. Mean daily maximum temperatures range from 30 to 40&#160;°C (86 to 104&#160;°F), except at higher elevations along the eastern seaboard, where the effects of a cold offshore current can be felt. In Mogadishu, for instance, average afternoon highs range from 28 to 32&#160;°C (82 to 90&#160;°F) in April. Some of the highest mean annual temperatures in the world have been recorded in the country; <a href="/wiki/Berbera" title="Berbera">Berbera</a> on the northwestern coast has an afternoon high that averages more than 38&#160;°C (100&#160;°F) from June through September. Nationally, mean daily minimums usually vary from about 15 to 30&#160;°C (59 to 86&#160;°F).<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> The greatest range in climate occurs in northern Somalia, where temperatures sometimes surpass 45&#160;°C (113&#160;°F) in July on the littoral plains and drop below the freezing point during December in the highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-ClimateSom_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ClimateSom-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In this region, relative humidity ranges from about 40% in the mid-afternoon to 85% at night, changing somewhat according to the season.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Unlike the climates of most other countries at this latitude, conditions in Somalia range from arid in the northeastern and central regions to <a href="/wiki/Semi-arid_climate" title="Semi-arid climate">semiarid</a> in the northwest and south. In the northeast, annual rainfall is less than 100&#160;mm (4&#160;in); in the central plateaus, it is about 200 to 300&#160;mm (8 to 12&#160;in). The northwestern and southwestern parts of the nation, however, receive considerably more rain, with an average of 510 to 610&#160;mm (20 to 24&#160;in) falling per year. Although the coastal regions are hot and humid throughout the year, the hinterland is typically dry and hot.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are four main seasons around which pastoral and agricultural life revolve, and these are dictated by shifts in the wind patterns. From December to March is the <i>Jilal</i>, the harshest dry season of the year. The main rainy season, referred to as the <i>Gu</i>, lasts from April to June. This period is characterized by the southwest monsoons, which rejuvenate the pasture land, especially the central plateau, and briefly transform the desert into lush vegetation. From July to September is the second dry season, the <i>Xagaa</i> (pronounced "Hagaa"). The <i>Dayr</i>, which is the shortest rainy season, lasts from October to December.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> The <i>tangambili</i> periods that intervene between the two monsoons (October–November and March–May) are hot and humid.<sup id="cite_ref-Hadden_191-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hadden-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Somalia" title="Wildlife of Somalia">Wildlife of Somalia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sambalaxx.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Sambalaxx.jpg/220px-Sambalaxx.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Sambalaxx.jpg/330px-Sambalaxx.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Sambalaxx.jpg/440px-Sambalaxx.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="500" /></a><figcaption>A camel in the northern mountains.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Somalia contains a variety of mammals due to its geographical and climatic diversity. Wildlife still occurring includes <a href="/wiki/Cheetah" title="Cheetah">cheetah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lion" title="Lion">lion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Reticulated_giraffe" title="Reticulated giraffe">reticulated giraffe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baboon" title="Baboon">baboon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serval" title="Serval">serval</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_bush_elephant" title="African bush elephant">elephant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bushpig" title="Bushpig">bushpig</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gazelle" title="Gazelle">gazelle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ibex" title="Ibex">ibex</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kudu" title="Kudu">kudu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dik-dik" title="Dik-dik">dik-dik</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oribi" title="Oribi">oribi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_wild_ass" title="Somali wild ass">Somali wild ass</a>, <a href="/wiki/Reedbuck" title="Reedbuck">reedbuck</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gr%C3%A9vy%27s_zebra" title="Grévy&#39;s zebra">Grévy's zebra</a>, <a href="/wiki/Elephant_shrew" title="Elephant shrew">elephant shrew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rock_hyrax" title="Rock hyrax">rock hyrax</a>, <a href="/wiki/Golden_mole" title="Golden mole">golden mole</a> and <a href="/wiki/Antelope" title="Antelope">antelope</a>. It also has a large population of the <a href="/wiki/Dromedary_camel" class="mw-redirect" title="Dromedary camel">dromedary camel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-mukasa81_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mukasa81-201">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somalia is home to around 727 species of birds. Of these, eight are endemic, one has been introduced by humans, and one is rare or accidental. Fourteen species are globally threatened. Birds species found exclusively in the country include the <i><a href="/wiki/Columba_oliviae" class="mw-redirect" title="Columba oliviae">Somali Pigeon</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Alaemon_hamertoni" class="mw-redirect" title="Alaemon hamertoni">Alaemon hamertoni</a></i> (Alaudidae), Lesser Hoopoe-Lark, <i><a href="/wiki/Heteromirafra_archeri" class="mw-redirect" title="Heteromirafra archeri">Heteromirafra archeri</a></i> (Alaudidae), Archer's Lark, <i><a href="/wiki/Mirafra_ashi" class="mw-redirect" title="Mirafra ashi">Mirafra ashi</a></i>, Ash's Bushlark, <i><a href="/wiki/Mirafra_somalica" class="mw-redirect" title="Mirafra somalica">Mirafra somalica</a></i> (Alaudidae), Somali Bushlark, <i><a href="/wiki/Spizocorys_obbiensis" class="mw-redirect" title="Spizocorys obbiensis">Spizocorys obbiensis</a></i> (Alaudidae), Obbia Lark, <i><a href="/wiki/Carduelis_johannis" class="mw-redirect" title="Carduelis johannis">Carduelis johannis</a></i> (Fringillidae), and Warsangli Linnet.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somalia's territorial waters are prime fishing grounds for highly migratory marine species, such as tuna. A narrow but productive continental shelf contains several <a href="/wiki/Demersal" class="mw-redirect" title="Demersal">demersal</a> fish and <a href="/wiki/Crustacean" title="Crustacean">crustacean</a> species.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Fish species found exclusively in the nation include <i>Cirrhitichthys randalli</i> (<a href="/wiki/Cirrhitidae" title="Cirrhitidae">Cirrhitidae</a>), <i>Symphurus fuscus</i> (<a href="/wiki/Cynoglossidae" class="mw-redirect" title="Cynoglossidae">Cynoglossidae</a>), <i>Parapercis simulata</i> OC (<a href="/wiki/Pinguipedidae" class="mw-redirect" title="Pinguipedidae">Pinguipedidae</a>), <i>Cociella somaliensis</i> OC (<a href="/wiki/Platycephalidae" title="Platycephalidae">Platycephalidae</a>), and <i>Pseudochromis melanotus</i> (<a href="/wiki/Pseudochromidae" class="mw-redirect" title="Pseudochromidae">Pseudochromidae</a>).
</p><p>There are roughly 235 species of reptiles. Of these, almost half live in the northern areas. Reptiles endemic to Somalia include the Hughes' <a href="/wiki/Saw-scaled_viper" class="mw-redirect" title="Saw-scaled viper">saw-scaled viper</a>, the Southern Somali garter snake, a racer (<i>Platyceps messanai</i>), a diadem snake (<i>Spalerosophis josephscorteccii</i>), the Somali <a href="/wiki/Sand_boa" class="mw-redirect" title="Sand boa">sand boa</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Angled_worm_lizard" title="Angled worm lizard">angled worm lizard</a>, a spiny-tailed lizard (<i>Uromastyx macfadyeni</i>), Lanza's agama, a gecko (<i>Hemidactylus granchii</i>), the Somali semaphore <a href="/wiki/Gecko" title="Gecko">gecko</a>, and a sand lizard (<a href="/wiki/Mesalina" title="Mesalina">Mesalina</a> or <a href="/wiki/Eremias" title="Eremias">Eremias</a>). A colubrid snake (<i>Aprosdoketophis andreonei</i>) and Haacke-Greer's skink (<i>Haackgreerius miopus</i>) are endemic species.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Politics_and_government">Politics and government</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Somalia" title="Politics of Somalia">Politics of Somalia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jawari.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Jawari.png/170px-Jawari.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Jawari.png/255px-Jawari.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Jawari.png/340px-Jawari.png 2x" data-file-width="505" data-file-height="574" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Mohamed_Osman_Jawari" title="Mohamed Osman Jawari">Mohamed Osman Jawari</a>, Former Speaker of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Parliament_of_Somalia" title="Federal Parliament of Somalia">Federal Parliament</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Somalia is a <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliamentary">parliamentary</a> <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democratic</a> <a href="/wiki/Republic" title="Republic">republic</a>. The <a href="/wiki/President_of_Somalia" title="President of Somalia">President of Somalia</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Head_of_state" title="Head of state">head of state</a> and <a href="/wiki/Commander-in-chief" title="Commander-in-chief">commander-in-chief</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Armed_Forces" title="Somali Armed Forces">Somali Armed Forces</a> and selects a <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Somalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Somalia">Prime Minister</a> to act as <a href="/wiki/Head_of_government" title="Head of government">head of government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Federal_Parliament_of_Somalia" title="Federal Parliament of Somalia">Federal Parliament of Somalia</a> is the national parliament of Somalia. The bicameral National Legislature consists of the House of the People (lower house) and the <a href="/wiki/Somalian_Senate" class="mw-redirect" title="Somalian Senate">Senate</a> (upper house), whose members are elected to serve four-year terms. The parliament elects the President, Speaker of Parliament and Deputy Speakers. It also has the authority to pass and veto laws.<sup id="cite_ref-Ajssihnp_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ajssihnp-206">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Judiciary_of_Somalia" title="Judiciary of Somalia">Judiciary of Somalia</a> is defined by the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Somalia" title="Constitution of Somalia">Provisional Constitution</a> of the Federal Republic of Somalia. Adopted on 1 August 2012 by a National Constitutional Assembly in Mogadishu,<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> the document was formulated by a committee of specialists chaired by attorney and Speaker of the Federal Parliament, <a href="/wiki/Mohamed_Osman_Jawari" title="Mohamed Osman Jawari">Mohamed Osman Jawari</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> It provides the legal foundation for the existence of the Federal Republic and source of legal authority.<sup id="cite_ref-Frspc_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frspc-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The national court structure is organized into three tiers: the Constitutional Court, Federal Government level courts and <a href="/wiki/States_and_regions_of_Somalia" title="States and regions of Somalia">State</a> level courts. A nine-member Judicial Service Commission appoints any Federal tier member of the judiciary. It also selects and presents potential Constitutional Court judges to the House of the People of the Federal Parliament for approval. If endorsed, the President appoints the candidate as a judge of the Constitutional Court. The five-member Constitutional Court adjudicates issues pertaining to the constitution, in addition to various Federal and sub-national matters.<sup id="cite_ref-Frspc_210-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frspc-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Somali law draws from a mixture of three different systems: <a href="/wiki/Civil_law_(legal_system)" title="Civil law (legal system)">civil law</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sharia" title="Sharia">Islamic law</a> and <a href="/wiki/Xeer" title="Xeer">customary law</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the collapse of Somalia in 1991, there were no relations or any contact between the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Somaliland" title="Government of Somaliland">Somaliland government</a>, which declared itself a country and the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Somalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Government of Somalia">government of Somalia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Somalia" title="Foreign relations of Somalia">Foreign relations of Somalia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_(16176887607).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg/220px-2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg/330px-2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg/440px-2015_01_25_Turkish_President_Visit_to_Somalia-1_%2816176887607%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3682" data-file-height="2450" /></a><figcaption>President of Somalia <a href="/wiki/Hassan_Sheikh_Mohamud" title="Hassan Sheikh Mohamud">Hassan Sheikh Mohamud</a> with Turkish President <a href="/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan" title="Recep Tayyip Erdoğan">Recep Tayyip Erdogan</a> opening the new terminal of Aden Abdulle International Airport in Mogadishu, Somalia. (25 January 2015)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Somalia's <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Somalia" title="Foreign relations of Somalia">foreign relations</a> are handled by the President as the head of state, the Prime Minister as the head of government, and the federal <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Somalia)" title="Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Somalia)">Ministry of Foreign Affairs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Frspc_210-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frspc-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mogturkemb.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Mogturkemb.jpg/220px-Mogturkemb.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Mogturkemb.jpg/330px-Mogturkemb.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Mogturkemb.jpg/440px-Mogturkemb.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="817" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkish</a> embassy in Mogadishu</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to Article 54 of the national constitution, the allocation of powers and resources between the Federal Government and the Federal Republic of Somalia's constituent Federal Member States shall be negotiated and agreed upon by the Federal Government and the Federal Member States, except in matters pertaining to foreign affairs, national defence, citizenship and immigration, and monetary policy. Article 53 also stipulates that the Federal Government shall consult the Federal Member States on major issues related to international agreements, including negotiations vis-a-vis foreign trade, finance and treaties.<sup id="cite_ref-Frspc_210-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frspc-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
The Federal Government maintains <a href="/wiki/Bilateralism" title="Bilateralism">bilateral relations</a> with a number of other central governments in the international community. Among these are <a href="/wiki/Djibouti%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Djibouti–Somalia relations">Djibouti</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Ethiopia–Somalia relations">Ethiopia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Egypt–Somalia relations">Egypt</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93United_Arab_Emirates_relations" title="Somalia–United Arab Emirates relations">United Arab Emirates</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93Yemen_relations" title="Somalia–Yemen relations">Yemen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93Turkey_relations" title="Somalia–Turkey relations">Turkey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Italy–Somalia relations">Italy</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations" title="Somalia–United Kingdom relations">United Kingdom</a>, <a href="/wiki/Denmark%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Denmark–Somalia relations">Denmark</a>, <a href="/wiki/France%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="France–Somalia relations">France</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Somalia–United States relations">United States</a>, the <a href="/wiki/China%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="China–Somalia relations">People's Republic of China</a>, <a href="/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Japan–Somalia relations">Japan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Somalia_relations" title="Russia–Somalia relations">Russian Federation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Somalia%E2%80%93South_Korea_relations" title="Somalia–South Korea relations">South Korea</a>.
</p><p>Additionally, Somalia has several <a href="/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_Somalia" title="List of diplomatic missions of Somalia">diplomatic missions</a> abroad. There are likewise various <a href="/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Somalia" title="List of diplomatic missions in Somalia">foreign embassies and consulates</a> based in the capital Mogadishu and elsewhere in the country.
</p><p>Somalia is also a member of many international organizations, such as the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, African Union and <a href="/wiki/Arab_League" title="Arab League">Arab League</a>. It was a founding member of the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a> in 1969.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Other memberships include the <a href="/wiki/African_Development_Bank" title="African Development Bank">African Development Bank</a>, <a href="/wiki/Group_of_77" title="Group of 77">Group of 77</a>, <a href="/wiki/Intergovernmental_Authority_on_Development" title="Intergovernmental Authority on Development">Intergovernmental Authority on Development</a>, <a href="/wiki/International_Bank_for_Reconstruction_and_Development" title="International Bank for Reconstruction and Development">International Bank for Reconstruction and Development</a>, <a href="/wiki/International_Civil_Aviation_Organization" title="International Civil Aviation Organization">International Civil Aviation Organization</a>, <a href="/wiki/International_Development_Association" title="International Development Association">International Development Association</a>, <a href="/wiki/International_Finance_Corporation" title="International Finance Corporation">International Finance Corporation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>, <a href="/wiki/World_Federation_of_Trade_Unions" title="World Federation of Trade Unions">World Federation of Trade Unions</a> and <a href="/wiki/World_Meteorological_Organization" title="World Meteorological Organization">World Meteorological Organization</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Somali_Armed_Forces" title="Somali Armed Forces">Somali Armed Forces</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_(16739095620).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg/220px-2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg/330px-2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg/440px-2015_03_25_SNA_PASSOUT-5_%2816739095620%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1064" /></a><figcaption>Instructors from the <a href="/wiki/European_Union_Training_Mission_in_Somalia" title="European Union Training Mission in Somalia">European Union Training Mission in Somalia</a> (EUTM) take Somali soldiers through training drills at Jazeera Training Camp in Mogadishu.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Somali_Armed_Forces" title="Somali Armed Forces">Somali Armed Forces</a> (SAF) are the military forces of the Federal Republic of Somalia.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Headed by the President as Commander in Chief, they are constitutionally mandated to ensure the nation's sovereignty, independence and territorial integrity.<sup id="cite_ref-Frspc_210-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frspc-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The SAF was initially made up of the <a href="/wiki/Somali_National_Army" title="Somali National Army">Army</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_Navy" title="Somali Navy">Navy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_Air_Force" title="Somali Air Force">Air Force</a>, <a href="/wiki/Somali_Police_Force" title="Somali Police Force">Police Force</a> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Security_Service_(Somalia)" title="National Security Service (Somalia)">National Security Service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In the post-independence period, it grew to become among the larger militaries on the continent.<sup id="cite_ref-Ramsbotham_222_146-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ramsbotham_222-146">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> The subsequent outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War" title="Somali Civil War">civil war</a> in 1991 led to the disbandment of the Somali National Army.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2004, the gradual process of reconstituting the military was put in motion with the establishment of the Transitional Federal Government (TFG). The Somali Armed Forces are now overseen by the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(Somalia)" title="Ministry of Defence (Somalia)">Ministry of Defence</a> of the Federal Government of Somalia, formed in mid-2012. In January 2013, the Somali federal government also re-opened the national intelligence service in Mogadishu, renaming the agency the <a href="/wiki/National_Intelligence_and_Security_Agency" class="mw-redirect" title="National Intelligence and Security Agency">National Intelligence and Security Agency</a> (NISA).<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_Armed_Forces" title="Somaliland Armed Forces">Somaliland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Puntland_Security_Force" title="Puntland Security Force">Puntland</a> regional governments maintain their own security and police forces.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Human_rights">Human rights</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Somalia" title="Human rights in Somalia">Human rights in Somalia</a></div>
<p>Both male and female <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Somalia" title="LGBT rights in Somalia">same-sex sexual activity</a> are <a href="/wiki/Capital_punishment_for_homosexuality" title="Capital punishment for homosexuality">punishable by death</a> within Somalia.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On October 3, 2020, a <a href="/wiki/UN" class="mw-redirect" title="UN">UN</a> human rights investigator raised concerns over Somali government's backtracking of human rights commitments. According to information collected by the investigator, Somali authorities were regressing on commitments to protect peoples' economic, social and <a href="/wiki/Cultural_rights" title="Cultural rights">cultural rights</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Somalia" title="Economy of Somalia">Economy of Somalia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg/220px-Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="139" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg/330px-Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg/440px-Air_Somalia_Tupolev_Tu-154.jpg 2x" data-file-width="826" data-file-height="521" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Air_Somalia" title="Air Somalia">Air Somalia</a> Tupolev <a href="/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-154" title="Tupolev Tu-154">Tu-154</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sharjah_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Sharjah (city)">Sharjah</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_Arab_Emirates" title="United Arab Emirates">United Arab Emirates</a>. Somalia today has several private airlines</figcaption></figure>
